RNA inverse folding can be solved in linear time for structures without isolated stacks or base pairs
Authors: Théo Boury, Samuel Gardelle, Laurent Bulteau, Yann Ponty
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
Inverse folding is a classic instance of negative RNA design which consists in finding a sequence that uniquely folds into a target secondary structure with respect to energy minimization. A breakthrough result of Bonnet et al. shows that, even in simple base pairs-based (BP) models, the decision version of a mildly constrained version of inverse folding is NP-hard. In this work, we show that inverse folding can be solved in linear time for a large collection of targets, including every structure that contains no isolated BP and no isolated stack (or, equivalently, when all helices consist of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^{+}$$\end{document}3+ base pairs). For structures featuring shorter helices, our linear algorithm is no longer guaranteed to produce a solution, but still does so for a large proportion of instances. Our approach introduces a notion of modulo m-separability, generalizing a property pioneered by Hales et al. Separability is a sufficient condition for the existence of a solution to the inverse folding problem. We show that, for any input secondary structure of length n, a modulo m-separated sequence can be produced in time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(n\,m\, 2^m)$$\end{document}O(nm2m) anytime such a sequence exists. Meanwhile, we show that any structure consisting of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3^{+}$$\end{document}3+ base pairs is either trivially non-designable, or always admits a modulo-2 separated solution. Solution sequences can thus be produced in linear time, and even be uniformly generated within the set of modulo-2 separable sequences.

Introduction

RNA inverse folding is a fascinating algorithmic problem which, given a target secondary structure T, consists of designing one or several sequences, all of which should uniquely fold into the target T according to a reference folding prediction algorithm. Considering a folding prediction algorithm as a mathematical function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi : \{A,C,G,U\}^\star \rightarrow \mathcal {S}\cup \{\perp \}$$\end{document}Φ:{A,C,G,U}⋆→S∪{⊥} mapping an RNA sequence to a unique predicted structure (or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\perp$$\end{document}⊥ if equally likely alternatives exist), inverse folding can be abstracted as the search for a preimage \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w\in \Phi ^{-1}(T)$$\end{document}w∈Φ-1(T) of the target structure T. This naturally generalizes into a variety of design tasks which, given a predictive algorithm implementing a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi$$\end{document}Φ, aim to create one or multiple instances predicted to behave in a certain way. Such a formulation is, in general, overly broad (e.g. it encompasses the concept of one-way functions in cryptography) to inspire reasonable hopes for a general solution. Still, a restriction of the inverse problem to certain types of computable functions/algorithms (e.g. amenable to dynamic programming) appears realistic and generally relevant to (synthetic) biology, yet poorly studied to this day.

In the specific case of RNA, despite being the object of substantial attention since its formal introduction in the early 1990s [1], the complexity of RNA inverse folding has remained elusive for almost three decades. A generalization of RNA inverse folding, including the energy model as part of the input, was shown to be NP-hard by Schnall-Levin et al. [2]. However, their reductions critically relied on (ab)using the energy model to encode a 3SAT instance, leaving the hardness of the problem largely open for a fixed energy model. The classic complexity of inverse folding was only settled, in 2020, when Bonnet et al. [3] finally showed the NP-hardness of RNA folding in a classic base pairs maximization setting. Such computational intractability (retrospectively) legitimizes a very large quantity of heuristic or exponential-time methods, based on local search [1, 4–7], bio-inspired metaheuristics [8–11], global sampling [12, 13], constraint programming [14, 15] and, more recently, neural networks-inspired generative models [16].

In parallel to complexity studies, Hales et al. [17] revisited the problem from a structural angle, attempting to characterize designable or undesignable families of secondary structures. The authors showed that saturated structures, having all positions paired, are designable if and only if their multiloop degrees do not exceed 4. They also introduced a notion of separability, a sufficient, yet not necessary in general, condition for a sequence to be a design for a given target. This notion allowed them to show that any target structure either features an occurrence of a locally-undesignable motif \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{m_{3\bullet },m_{5}\}$$\end{document}{m3∙,m5}, or can always be transformed into a separable structure by adding at most one base pair per helix. More strikingly, they proposed linear-time algorithms for producing a single solution for each characterized class of designable structures, painting a – puzzling – contrasted picture of general hardness (as per Bonnet et al. [3]) and practical facility for inverse folding.

In this work, we further those studies and show that:While conceptually elegant, we show that separability unfortunately remains challenging: Finding a separated design for a given structure is NP-hard, even when restricted to structures avoiding isolated base pairs;Conversely, we prove that any structure with helices of length greater than 3 base pairs is either trivially not designable (i.e. contains \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{m_{3\bullet },m_{5}\}$$\end{document}{m3∙,m5}), or separable. Moreover, if designable, a solution sequence can then be designed in linear-time. This constraint is relevant to the objectives of RNA design, as targeted secondary structures are typically stable and tend to avoid shorter – unstable – helices;To establish this result, we introduce of the concept of modulo m-separability, a refined version of separability, which coincides with general separability upon setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m\ge n/2$$\end{document}m≥n/2. Deciding m-separability clearly remains NP-hard in general, but it can be solved (+ a solution sequence be produced) in time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(n\, 2^m)$$\end{document}O(n2m) by a Fixed-Parameter Tractable (FPT) algorithm for m;We prove that this algorithm solves all instances of inverse folding with minimal helix lengths of 3 BPs when invoked with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=2$$\end{document}m=2 and, even in this restricted setting, many instances with shorter helices;We adapt our algorithm into a uniform random generator of separated designs, combining a mildly unambiguous dynamic programming scheme with a rejection strategy that achieves an average-case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(n\,m\,2^m)$$\end{document}O(nm2m) time complexity;Finally, we empirically observe that m-separated sequences often represent solutions for instances featuring isolated base pairs or stacks. Moreover, despite being only guaranteed to represent designs with respect to base pair maximization, are also likely to represent designs in the more realistic Turner energy model and, in a relaxed setting, are also superior than mere compatible sequences for multiloops of larger cardinalities. Finally, we observe that m-separated sequences seem to offer sufficient diversity to enable a control of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {GC}\%$$\end{document}GC%.

Without loss of generality, a secondary structure can be represented as a tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=(V(T), E(T))$$\end{document}T=(V(T),E(T)), whose nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V(T)$$\end{document}V(T) are in bijection with base pairs (internal nodes1) and unpaired regions (leaves), and whose edges represent the inclusion of base pairs. Given a node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in V(T)$$\end{document}v∈V(T), we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {parent}(v)$$\end{document}parent(v) the parent of v in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T, and by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {children}(v)$$\end{document}children(v) the list of children of v in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T. A loop is the subtree restricted to a node and its (direct) children. The tree is rooted in a special \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {Root}$$\end{document}Root node, associated with the whole sequence interval. An helix of length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell$$\end{document}ℓ of the tree is a maximal path \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_1,\ldots , v_\ell$$\end{document}v1,…,vℓ of base pair nodes such that each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_i$$\end{document}vi with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i<\ell$$\end{document}i<ℓ has a single child \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{i+1}$$\end{document}vi+1 (no leaf attached). A helix of length 1 is an isolated base pair. A helix of length 2 is an isolated stack. We define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{\min }$$\end{document}hmin as the minimum length over all helices of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T. As the target tree is always explicit and unmodified through proofs and algorithms we do not specify it explicitly in the notations.

RNA inverse folding considers a target secondary structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T, and constructs a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \in \Sigma ^n$$\end{document}ω∈Σn whose unique base-pair maximizing secondary structure is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T.

Problem 1

Inverse_FoldingBP

Input: Target secondary structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T, sequence length n

Output: Sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w\in \Sigma ^n$$\end{document}w∈Σn satisfying both:Compatibility with target structure: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\in \mathcal {S}_{w}$$\end{document}T∈Sw;Uniqueness of the target as the optimal fold for the sequence: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\forall T' \in \mathcal {S}_{w}, T'\ne T, |T'|<|T|.$$\end{document}∀T′∈Sw,T′≠T,|T′|<|T|.or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\perp$$\end{document}⊥ if no such sequence exists.

Compatibility with target structure: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\in \mathcal {S}_{w}$$\end{document}T∈Sw;

Compatibility with target structure: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\in \mathcal {S}_{w}$$\end{document}T∈Sw;

Uniqueness of the target as the optimal fold for the sequence: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\forall T' \in \mathcal {S}_{w}, T'\ne T, |T'|<|T|.$$\end{document}∀T′∈Sw,T′≠T,|T′|<|T|.

Uniqueness of the target as the optimal fold for the sequence: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\forall T' \in \mathcal {S}_{w}, T'\ne T, |T'|<|T|.$$\end{document}∀T′∈Sw,T′≠T,|T′|<|T|.

Nevertheless, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsc{Inverse\_Folding}}_\text {BP}$$\end{document}INVERSE_FOLDINGBP, mildly extended to allow further restrictions on individual sequence positions, was shown to be NP-hard by Bonnet et al. [3]. (The used restriction requires the inclusion of some constraints of the form “nucleotide i must be labeled by the base letter b”)

A sequence is called a design for a structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T if it represents a solution to the inverse folding problem for the input \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T. Note that the uniqueness condition can be tested in polynomial time using a variant of the Nussinov algorithm [17, 18]. In addition to showing that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsc{Inverse\_Folding}}_\text {BP}$$\end{document}INVERSE_FOLDINGBP is in NP, such an algorithm enables, for moderate sequence lengths, a systematic folding of all sequences in order to characterize the set of structures admitting a solution. For instance, Fig. 1 shows that, while only 2.4% of RNA sequences of length 12 represent a design for some target, roughly half of the secondary structure admits at least one solution sequence, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\approx 49$$\end{document}≈49 on average, for the inverse folding problem.Fig. 1Exhaustive designability analysis of 12nts RNA sequences/structures. (Left) For a minimum base pair span of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta =0$$\end{document}θ=0, there exists 15 511 sary structures over 12 nucleotides, of which little over half (8 111) admits at least a solution to the inverse folding problem. (Right) The number of valid solutions varies substantially between targets and appears to depend on the number of base pairs. Overall, out of the 16 777 216 RNA sequences of length 12, only 399 348 (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\approx 2.4\%$$\end{document}≈2.4%) represent a valid design for some structureFig. 2Forbidden motifs. Motifs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{5}$$\end{document}m5 (left) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{3\bullet }$$\end{document}m3∙ (right), both shown as a tree (with a, b, c d, e arbitrary integers) and as nested base-pairs. Note that the relative order of the children base-pairs and the leaf in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{3\bullet }$$\end{document}m3∙ pattern is irrelevant. Note that any such undesignable structure remains undesignable upon permuting children, or inserting new ones

Forbidden motifs. Motifs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{5}$$\end{document}m5 (left) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{3\bullet }$$\end{document}m3∙ (right), both shown as a tree (with a, b, c d, e arbitrary integers) and as nested base-pairs. Note that the relative order of the children base-pairs and the leaf in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{3\bullet }$$\end{document}m3∙ pattern is irrelevant. Any assignment of base pair letters (either matching a proper coloring of the tree or not) leads to a possible local rerooting of at least two base pairs yielding an alternative thus making the structure undesignable [17]. Note that any such undesignable structure remains undesignable upon permuting children, or inserting new ones

We remind that, as noted by Halès et al. [17], two key motifs are not designable in a base pair maximization setting, see Fig. 2:The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{5}$$\end{document}m5 motif consists of 5 base pairs occurring on the same loop (not counting the Root). No sequence can be designed for such a motif, since exposing 5 base pairs on a loop always allows for local refolding to have the same number of base pairs. This follows from the inspection of Fig. 3, where the largest set of mutually compatible base pairs clearly has cardinality 4;The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{3\bullet }$$\end{document}m3∙ motif consists of 3 base pairs (excluding the Root) and one unpaired position. Indeed, as shown in Fig. 3, the presence of an unpaired nucleotide either forbids the co-occurrence of any adjacent base pair (G or U), or only allows three (C or A). Since at most two of those base pairs can co-occur in a successful loop design, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{3\bullet }$$\end{document}m3∙ is not designable.Any occurrence of these structures (or any other undesignable structure [19]) as a subgraph of an instance makes the instance undesignable. In particular, the presence of a node with 4+ base-pair children, or 3+ children including two base-pairs and a leaf, leads to an undesignable structure.Fig. 3Local design rules. Inverse folding as a tree coloring problem

We start by reminding the coloring framework introduced by Halès et al. [17].

Definition 1

(Coloring) A coloring of a (secondary structure) tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T is a function associating a color to each node (except the root and the leaves which always get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varnothing$$\end{document}∅).

A coloring of a tree T typically induces multiple RNA sequences that are compatible with, but not guaranteed to fold into, the given secondary structure through letters assignment rules. Namely, in any sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w$$\end{document}w derived from a coloring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ, we have for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i,j)\in T$$\end{document}(i,j)∈T:If ;If ;If For nodes, the freedom in choosing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textsf {A}, \textsf {U})$$\end{document}(A,U) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textsf {U},\textsf {A})$$\end{document}(U,A) depends on the context: the choice may be unconstrained (e.g. when isolated within a helix), or forced (e.g. when two gray nodes are involved in a multiloop or stack). However, this property will only impact the number of sequences associated with the coloring, but bears no consequence on the existence of a solution to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textsc{Inverse-Folding}}_\text {BP}$$\end{document}INVERSE-FOLDINGBP, since the problem asks for the production of a single sequence.Fig. 4A proper coloring is necessary towards design. In (A), having two children implies that the sequence derived from this coloring features a motif where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {G}$$\end{document}G and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {C}$$\end{document}C can reconfigure locally. In that case, they form an alternative structure that contains the same number of base pairs. Conversely, in (B), the proper coloring ensures that locally no alternative of equal (or better) energy exists by forcing some consecutive incompatibilities

A proper coloring is necessary towards design. In (A), having two children implies that the sequence derived from this coloring features a motif where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {G}$$\end{document}G and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {C}$$\end{document}C can reconfigure locally. In that case, they form an alternative structure that contains the same number of base pairs. Conversely, in (B), the proper coloring ensures that locally no alternative of equal (or better) energy exists by forcing some consecutive incompatibilities

Denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{c}$$\end{document}c¯ the inverse of a color c, defined as , and . Denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|C|_{c}$$\end{document}|C|c the number of occurrences of color c in vector C.

Definition 2

(Proper Coloring) A coloring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ is proper when, for each node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V(T)$$\end{document}v∈V(T), the vector of colors C, composed of the complementary color of the node concatenated with the colors of its children, respects the following constraints:

The use of the complementary color of v in C enables a compact definition: it forbids and to have respectively and children which would result in an alternative rerooting of the pairs. These conditions must also hold for the colorless \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {Root}$$\end{document}Root, but with C being restricted to the colors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {children}(\textsf {Root})$$\end{document}children(Root).

In terms of RNA design, the proper condition is necessary for an associated sequence to be a solution to inverse folding. Indeed, any coloring that is not proper will be associated with sequences that can be locally reconfigured, this without losing any base pair (see Fig. 4 for local competing structures). (Fig. 5).Fig. 5(1) 2D and dot-bracket representations of a secondary structure. Helices of sizes respectively 1 (isolated base pairs), 2 (isolated stacks) and more than 3 are represented in light red, purple and blue. (2) Same secondary structure as a tree. The tree is colored and levels are represented in red and blue bubbles. The coloring is proper but non-separated as the level of the leaf 19 is the same as the level of the node 41-49. A non-separated coloring is not guaranteed to induce a design for its target, but may still do so, as is the case here. (3) Same secondary structure, colored in a separated (necessarily proper) manner. This coloring yields one or multiple designs (depending on the choice of AU or UA for nodes). Notably, this coloring is 2-separated, as leaves and nodes end up at odd and even levels respectively

(1) 2D and dot-bracket representations of a secondary structure. Helices of sizes respectively 1 (isolated base pairs), 2 (isolated stacks) and more than 3 are represented in light red, purple and blue. (2) Same secondary structure as a tree. The tree is colored and levels are represented in red and blue bubbles. The coloring is proper but non-separated as the level of the leaf 19 is the same as the level of the node 41-49. A non-separated coloring is not guaranteed to induce a design for its target, but may still do so, as is the case here. (3) Same secondary structure, colored in a separated (necessarily proper) manner. This coloring yields one or multiple designs (depending on the choice of AU or UA for nodes). Notably, this coloring is 2-separated, as leaves and nodes end up at odd and even levels respectively

Definition 3

(Levels) Given a coloring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ of a tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T, the level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L: V(T) \rightarrow \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}L:V(T)→Z of a node v is where p denotes the color vector associated with the shortest node sequence from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {parent}(v)$$\end{document}parent(v) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {Root}$$\end{document}Root.

On an RNA level, the concept of level helps categorize, and possibly control, the set of alternative structures to the target. Indeed, consider a sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w$$\end{document}w generated from a coloring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ. First remark that, in order for an alternative structure to be competitive, every occurrence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {C}$$\end{document}C must be paired. Whenever two positions i and j interact to form a base pair, it can be shown that the inner interval ]i, j[ interval contains \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(i)-L(j)$$\end{document}L(i)-L(j) more \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {G}$$\end{document}G than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {C}$$\end{document}C. Meanwhile the outermost interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[1,i[\,\cup \, ]j,n]$$\end{document}[1,i[∪]j,n] features the opposite imbalance (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(i)-L(j)$$\end{document}L(i)-L(j) more \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {C}$$\end{document}C than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {G}$$\end{document}G). In other words, any structure that contains a base pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i,j)\notin T$$\end{document}(i,j)∉T already has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\times |L(i)-L(j)|$$\end{document}2×|L(i)-L(j)| fewer base pairs than the target structure. Thus only structures made of pairs (i, j) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(i)=L(j)$$\end{document}L(i)=L(j) need to be considered as viable alternatives to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T. This property can be exploited as a design principle, as formalized by the following property.

Definition 4

(Separated coloring) A coloring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ is separated for a target \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T if and only if it is proper and the levels of -colored nodes and leaves do not overlap:

This immediately suggests a design strategy that associates A to unpaired positions and assigns and colors such that nodes end up as different levels as the leaves. Indeed, in this setting, Hales et al. [17] showed that the proper coloring of a saturated structure (without unpaired position) yields a sequence that uniquely folds with respect to base pair maximization. It follows that a competitive/alternative structure may only result from a base pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(i,j)\notin T$$\end{document}(i,j)∉T, a position of which is a node while the other is a leaf. Ensuring that all nodes and leaves are found at different levels is thus sufficient to guarantee the designability of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T, i.e. the existence of a solution to this instance of the inverse folding problem.

More generally, we say that a target secondary structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T is separable if there exists a coloring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ is separated for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T. We recall the main results of Halès et al. [17] here.

Theorem 1

(Separable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\implies$$\end{document}⇒ Designable [17]) If a tree/secondary structure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T is separable, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T is designable.

Moreover, given a separated coloring, an RNA sequence that uniquely folds into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T, i.e. a solution to the inverse folding problem, can be found in linear time.

Remark 1

Note that any design sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w$$\end{document}w, generated through a separated coloring, avoids any alternative structure featuring GU base pair(s). Indeed, every G and C need to be paired to achieve the number of base pairs featured in the MFE. Meanwhile, the formation of any GU base pair, leaves one C and one A unpaired, resulting in the overall loss of at least one base pair. Structures featuring GU base pairs can thus be safely ignored.

Remark 2

Note that an alternative assignment of letters would be C to the unpaired positions, UA for nodes, AU for nodes. It has no impact thanks to the symmetry of the base pair compatibility graph as depicted on Fig. 3. In practice, it gives access if desired to double the number of sequences with the ones with the unpaired position at C that could have a slightly different content in terms of G and C even if not studied in this manuscript.

Separability: intrinsic and computational limits

Despite utilizing separability to explore a design of approximative instances, the work of Halès et al. [17] left open the complexity of searching for a separated coloring, as well as the existence of designable, yet non-separable, structures. An exhaustive search for all structures with up to 12 bases, summarized in Fig. 1, shows that for such small instances, all designable instances are separable.

Designable structures may not be separable

We first show that non-separable designable instances can be constructed.Fig. 6Designability does not imply separability. Left: A target structure that does not admit any separated coloring instance. Proposition 2

(Designable Separable) There exists a target structure which: (i) does not admit a separated coloring; and (ii) admits a solution to the inverse folding problem.

Proof

We use the tree T of Fig. 6 as a counterexample to the notion that separability fully captures designability. First, note that a separated coloring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ of T would be extremely constrained. Node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$5-18$$\end{document}5-18 should be and the nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2-4$$\end{document}2-4 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$19-21$$\end{document}19-21 are and respectively, or vice-versa due to their respective leaf. Thus, we have two leaves at levels 1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-1$$\end{document}-1. At least, one of the two children of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$5-18$$\end{document}5-18, w.l.o.g \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$6-7$$\end{document}6-7 is or . One child of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$6-7$$\end{document}6-7 is then necessarily , leading to a child of level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+1$$\end{document}+1 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-1$$\end{document}-1. With two leaves at level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+1$$\end{document}+1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-1$$\end{document}-1, a direct consequence is that T is non-separable.

Now, we show that T is designable. We propose the sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w$$\end{document}w of Fig. 6. Using a simple dynamic programming algorithm, it is possible to check that the best folding for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w$$\end{document}w is unique and corresponds to the secondary structure encoded as the tree T. Intuitively, the only competitive alternative base pair is the one corresponding to the overlap of the levels. It consists of joining the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {U}$$\end{document}U from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$8-9$$\end{document}8-9 with the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {A}$$\end{document}A at position 3. By doing so, note that the base pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$5-18$$\end{document}5-18 will be disconnected with no way to pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {A}$$\end{document}A with another \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf {U}$$\end{document}U due to the connection between 5 and 7. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}□

Notice that, despite not being separated, the coloring shown in Fig. 6 is compatible with a sequence that is a design for its target. This illustrates the fact that, while not being guaranteed to uniquely fold as their intended target, sequences produced from non-separated colorings may still represent solutions for the inverse folding problem.Fig. 7Main gadget used to build non-separable instances with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{\min } =2$$\end{document}hmin=2. Structures with small helices may not be separable

Proposition 3

There exist non-separable structures with minimum helix length of 2 (i.e. such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{\min } =2$$\end{document}hmin=2).

The full proof relies on a counterexample built from the gadget in Fig. 7 and is given in the next paragraph. Intuitively, T(a, b) saturates all levels modulo b with leaves, so that none remains available for nodes. Meanwhile, the presence of multiloops forces proper colorings to use nodes, so a collision occurs and the gadget is not m-separable for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m\le b$$\end{document}m≤b. By assembling 5 copies of T(a, b) with large b and increasing values of a, we obtain a target that is not separable for any m.

We start with the following remark:

Proposition 4

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0,\ldots , u_k$$\end{document}u0,…,uk is a path in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T and each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_i$$\end{document}ui for even i has a leaf attached to it then, for any coloring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ of the path, we have and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (u_i)=\chi (u_0)$$\end{document}χ(ui)=χ(u0) for all i.

Proof

Indeed, by the proper coloring constraint, every node with an attached leaf or with a leaf sibling may not be , so all for all i. Moreover, there can be no direct edge between and nodes, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (u_i)=\chi (u_{i-1})$$\end{document}χ(ui)=χ(ui-1) for all i which gives the desired property by induction. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}□

With this gadget in hand, we can now build a non-separable structure with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{\min }=2$$\end{document}hmin=2.

Proof of proposition 3

We build a non-separable instance I without size-1 helix nor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(m_{3\bullet }, m_{5})$$\end{document}(m3∙,m5) motif. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\ge 2$$\end{document}a≥2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b\ge 2$$\end{document}b≥2 be even numbers. Let T(a, b) be the gadget from Fig 7, containing a length-a path from the root to an internal node denoted t, and three length-b branches attached to t. Further attach a leaf to every node at an even distance from the root (except t itself). Note that all helices in T(a, b) have length 2. The level of a copy of some T(a, b) gadget is the level reached under node t of this gadget.

We build the instance I as a tree containing 5 copies of the gadget T(a, b), precisely \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I=( ((T[10,100], T[20,100])), ((T[30,100], T[40,100])), T[50,100])$$\end{document}I=(((T[10,100],T[20,100])),((T[30,100],T[40,100])),T[50,100]).

First note that for a copy of gadget T(a, b) at level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell$$\end{document}ℓ in any separable coloring, there is anode at level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell$$\end{document}ℓ, since the node t has three children and at least one must be . Also, there exists two integers u, v such that, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in [ 1,b[$$\end{document}x∈[1,b[, there is a leaf at level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell +ux$$\end{document}ℓ+ux if x is odd, and level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell +vx$$\end{document}ℓ+vx if x is even. Indeed, pick one gray child U of t, and one non-gray child V. All vertices under U form an all-white or all-black branch by Proposition 4(we let respectively \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u=-1$$\end{document}u=-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u=1$$\end{document}u=1), and vertices at levels \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l+u, l+3u, \ldots , l+bu$$\end{document}l+u,l+3u,…,l+bu (or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l+(b-1)u$$\end{document}l+(b-1)u) have a pending leaf. From the above, if there arenodes at levels \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _1$$\end{document}ℓ1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _2$$\end{document}ℓ2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell -b\le \ell _1<\ell <\ell _2 \le \ell +b$$\end{document}ℓ-b≤ℓ1<ℓ<ℓ2≤ℓ+b, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _1\ne \ell _2\mod 2$$\end{document}ℓ1≠ℓ2mod2 (since otherwise, one of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _1,\ell _2$$\end{document}ℓ1,ℓ2 could be written as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell +ux$$\end{document}ℓ+ux with even x, so that level would be a leaf level).

Aiming at a contradiction, assume that I admits a separable coloring. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _1\le \ell _2\le \ell _3\le \ell _4\le \ell _5$$\end{document}ℓ1≤ℓ2≤ℓ3≤ℓ4≤ℓ5 be the levels of all five copies of the T[a, b] gadgets of I, in ascending order. \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}□

Computational hardness of deciding separability

Regarding computational complexity, although looking for a separable coloring is not directly equivalent to finding a design for a structure, we show that this decision problem (formalized below) is also NP-complete.

Problem 2

Separability

Input: Target tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T (without any occurrence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{3\bullet }$$\end{document}m3∙ or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{5}$$\end{document}m5 motif)

Output: Coloring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ of the tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ is separated

Theorem 5

Separability is NP-complete.

We further show that even when isolated base pairs are forbidden in the input structure (e.g. helices are all of size 2 or more), the separability problem is still NP-hard. Thus, unless \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P=NP$$\end{document}P=NP, the hope to find a polynomial algorithm for separability holds only when helices are of size 3 or more:

Problem 3

2-Helix Separability

Input: Target tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T (without any occurrence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{3\bullet }$$\end{document}m3∙ or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{5}$$\end{document}m5 motif) whose corresponding target structure contains no isolated base pair (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{\min }=2$$\end{document}hmin=2)

Output: Coloring \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ of the tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T$$\end{document}T such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}χ is separated

Theorem 6

2-helix separability is NP-complete.

Clearly, Theorem 5 follows from Theorem 6, since the latter relates to a strictly more general problem. We first give an outline of the reduction below, then provide the full proof in the following subsections.

Although proper colorability is a local constraint, separability implies a form of synchronization between different branches of the tree, since a conflict can appear between a leaf and a node even if they are in remote sections of the tree. However, we do not have a direct way to enforce that a specific level has nodes or leaves, since there is a lot of freedom in proper coloring constraints, especially in trees with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{\min }=2$$\end{document}hmin=2. The first building block for our reduction is a blocking gadget that saturates one parity (either odd or even levels) in some interval with leaves. This is matched with a constant-size synchronization gadget where two levels of different parities necessarily have nodes. So, even if both gadgets are present in different branches, they must be placed at different levels.

Using these two gadgets, we build our reduction from Bin Packing with a tree using one branch per item. Each item has a blocking gadget having the size of the item surrounded by two synchronization gadgets. This enforces that items must be packed in non-overlapping ranges of levels. Additional synchronization gadgets further enforce that series of consecutive items sum up to the target bin size, thus enforcing that items are ordered according to a correct bin packing. However, the synchronization gadget induces some margin of freedom on the specific position of the levels, so consecutive items may be misaligned by some constant margin. This leads to a formulation of Bin Packing as an interval packing problem, with blurred endpoints with some constant margin L.

Formulation of bin packing as L-blurred interval packing

2-helix separability is clearly in NP, since any coloring (certificate) can be checked in linear time. We prove hardness by reduction from bin packing which we formulate as an interval packing problem using unary encoding and blurred endpoints.

Reduction from interval packing to separability

The reduction is based on two gadgets called blocking and synchronization gadgets. The first gives a long chain of nodes with a leaf attached to every other node; we show that this enforces a long interval of levels with leaves at all odd or even level. The second has a fixed size, and is incompatible with a blocking gadget since any separated coloring has both an odd and an even level. Both gadgets are defined in the following paragraphs, as well as their main properties. These properties are formulated in terms of synchronized and blocked levels, defined now.

We set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H=12$$\end{document}H=12 (chosen as the height of the synchronization gadget defined below), and use a blur value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L=3H+2$$\end{document}L=3H+2.

A level u is H-synchronized if there are two levels with different parity in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[u-H,u+H]$$\end{document}[u-H,u+H].

A level u is H-blocked if either all odd or all even levels in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[u-H,u+H]$$\end{document}[u-H,u+H] are leaf levels.

Observation 1

In any separated coloring, no level can be both H-synchronized and H-blocked.

Blocking gadget

A blocking gadget of size q in a tree is a chain of q node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_1,\ldots , s_q$$\end{document}s1,…,sq with a leaf attached to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_i$$\end{document}si for each odd i.

Proposition 7

In any proper coloring of a size-q blocking gadget, all nodes have the same or color. Furthermore, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _1,\ell _2$$\end{document}ℓ1,ℓ2, be the levels above the root and below the last node of the chain, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _1\le \ell _2$$\end{document}ℓ1≤ℓ2. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _2=\ell _1+q$$\end{document}ℓ2=ℓ1+q and all levels in interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\ell _1+H,\ell _2-H]$$\end{document}[ℓ1+H,ℓ2-H] are H-blocked.

Proof

By the proper coloring condition, no parent or sibling of a leaf can be , and 2 adjacent non- nodes must have the same color, so all nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_i$$\end{document}si are given the same non- color. Furthermore, if the gadget is colored , then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _1$$\end{document}ℓ1 is the level above the root, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _2=\ell _1+q$$\end{document}ℓ2=ℓ1+q. Plus, there are leaves at levels \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _1 + 2i+1$$\end{document}ℓ1+2i+1 for all i such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le 2i+1\le q$$\end{document}1≤2i+1≤q, so all levels j with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _1+H\le j\le \ell _2-H$$\end{document}ℓ1+H≤j≤ℓ2-H are H-blocked. Synchronization gadget
